THE STORY BEHIND THE B-2 BOMBER
By Rick Atkinson
Waahington Post Staff Writer
LOS ANGELES
I rail and dying at the age of 85, John
than a brown suit full of bones as he
shambled into a defense plant conference
room on a January day in 1981. The
occasion was both sentimental and deeply
classified: The Air Force had agreed to
make Jack Northrop privy to one of the
nation's most secret military ventures, a
revolutionary bomber soon to be
code-named "Project Senior C.J.*
The old man signed a secrecy oath and
sat down at a table, bracketed by a half
dozen executives of the company bearing
his name-Northrop Corp. An Air Force
colonel ticked off the proposed bomber's
vital statistics: range, speed, payload and,
most important, its stealthy ability to elude
radar. If successful, the bomber could turn
the $250 billion Soviet air defense system
into junk, a comforting prospect, given
renewed tension between the superpowers.
Northrop listened intently, knotting his
hands together to control their trembling.
Someone hoisted a large wooden box from
the floor onto the table. Opening the lid,
Northrop saw an 18-inch model shaped like
a boomerang, with no tail and only the
faintest hint of a fuselage. It bore an
uncanny resemblance to the YB-49, a
"flying wing bomber that Jack Northrop
had designed and built in the late 1940s
only to have the Air Force declare the plane
unstable, withdraw its support and order all
existing YB-49s chopped into scrap.
Heartbroken, Jack Northrop had resigned
from his company in 1952, an aerospace
genius reduced to self-imposed exile from
the industry he had helped create.
Northrop's eyes reddened and flooded
with tears as he stared at this reincarnation
of his flying wing. Choking back a soo, ne
whispered, "Now I know why God has kept
me alive for the last 25 years."
A month later, on Feb. 18, 1981, Jack
Northrop died.

REDESIGNING THE WING
n 1983, Northrop Corp. and
the Air Force concluded that
the stealth bomber needed a
radical redesign to withstand
stresses of flying
long
distances
at
low altitude.
n addition to strengthening
the plane internally, the new
design-which took a year
and cost $1 billion-sharply
altered the wing's "trail-
ing edgeạẠạto provide
more
control
surfaces.
A year later, in 1984, the job was finished. The trail-
ing edge of the new wing looked markedly different,
with a sawtooth shape that increased the control sur-
faces of the airplane to improve stability.
But at a price. Not only had the redesign cost $1 bil
lion, but the extensive work inside the wing meant that
electrical and hydraulic systems also had to be reposi-
tioned.

{"alpha_words": 853, "chars": 5673, "method": "ocr", "page": 191, "variant": "plain-vision"}
SUPPLEMENT: FRIDAY, October 13, 1989
The Washington Post
ОСт. 9, 1989
Pg. 1 :
Unraveling Stealth's 'Black World'
Questions of Cost and Mission Arise Amid Debate Over Secrecy
Second of three articles
By Rick Atkinson
Washungton Post Staff Writer
LOS ANGELES
O
I April 8, 1982, the Northrop Corp. made an
unusual purchase for an aerospace firm: the
company bought itself an automobile factory.
For $65 million, Northrop took over an abandoned
Ford assembly plant in the Los Angeles suburb of
Pico Rivera. Workmen hauled away the hulks of
half-built Thunderbirds, then attacked the building
with acetylene torches-and fire extinguishers to
douse the resulting blazes. Within months, the old
paint bays and assembly lines were gone, a new
three-story engineering center had risen next to the
plant, and Pico Rivera-or limply "Peek*-had been
converted into a top-secret military plant.
On Monday, Jan. 3, 1983, Peek opened for
business. Four thousand employees-many of whom
had been working at Northrop's clandestine "Building
A-Y° near the Los Angeles airport-reported to their
new work stations. With the new year and the new
plant came a renewed sense of excitement: many
workers felt that they had been directly challenged
by Fresident Reagan to help protect the nation by
building a revolutionary weapon, the B-2 stealth
bomber, code-named Project Senior C.J.
In the 15 months since winning the bomber
contract in late 1981, Northrop had been feverishly
busy-hiring, building, reorganizing the company to
create a new Advanced Systems Division. Major
subcontractors, including several of the largest
defense firms in the country, had been recruited:
LTV of Texas to handle the titanium frame and inner
wing, General Electric to build the engines, and
Boeing to construct the immense outer wings.
With each passing month, Northrop engineers felt
more certain that they were close to unlocking the
secrets of stealth. In deepest secrecy, the company
experimented with a one-quarter scale mini-bomber
code-named Tacit Blue (and nicknamed The Whale
because of its leviathan profile); radar testa
confirmed that theoretical computer calculations
were within 3 percent of the actual "signatures," or
radar reflections. Northrop now believed more firmly
than ever that a bomber could elude short- and
medium-length radar waves used by missiles and jet
fighters. The plane would not be invisible; longer
wavelength radars, such as those used in huge early
warning (EW) systems, might detect the bomber at
great distances. But Air Force and Northrop experts
concluded that for many years the enemy would be
unable to "hand off" the EW information to the smaller
radars needed to pinpoint, follow and destroy the plane.
At Pico Rivera the company had built the largest
computer facility west of the Mississippi River, provid-
ing engineers with a revolutionary computer graphics
system that permitted three-dimensional scrutiny of the
plane's 400,000 parts. The system included redundant
memory banks in Texas and Seattle. Northrop was tak-
ing no chances that a California earthquake would wipe
out its engineers' hard work.
All of this, of course, was undertaken in theẠblack
worldạof utter secrecy, known as SAR, Special Access
Required. Workers labored "in honorable exile, as
Northrop chairman Thomas V. Jones later put it, unable
to tell family or friends anything about their work.
At government insistence, minor subcontractors-
not knowing that they were making widgets for a
stealth bomber-often shipped parts to phony front
companies; unmarked trucks then hauled the freight to
Pico Rivera at night. Military officers visiting Peek
wore civilian clothes, and VIP helicopters touched down
elsewhere to avoid arousing suspicion.
Because of concern about electronic eavesdropping
by Soviet trawlers offshore, computers remained in
metal-lined rooms. Many computer terminals also were
shrouded with curtains to deter prying co-workers. Be-
fore leaving their stations to smoke a cigarette or use
the bathroom, workers in particularly sensitive areas
had to lock everything in a safe. One vigilant woman,
hearing the click of what sounded like a small camera in
an adjacent restroom stall, immediately called for help;
security forces burst in on the suspect to find not a spy
microfilming documents, but a mortified employee bal-
ancing her checkbook with a pocket calculator.
So tight was security that one vice president who had
been with Northrop for years had to persuade his Ca-
nadian wife to become an American citizen before he
could be "read into the stealth program. Beginning in
1981, the government also conducted hundreds of poly-
graph examinations among those working on the B-2
and other secret projects, looking initially for spies and
later for drug users as well.
Such extraordinary measures cost money. Knowledge-
able sources estimate that security accounts for 10 to 15
percent of Lockheed's F-117 stealth fighter costs and 3
percent of the B-2; the SAR procedures alone are expect-
ed to cost about $520 million over the life of the B-2.
Tom Jones later joked that he had been forbidden
even to dream about the secret bomber, but the Air
Force displayed no sense of humor about security. In
1982, convinced that Northrop was too lackadaisical,
several government officials flew to Los Angeles for an
all-day "come to Jesus meeting." as one participant
dubbed it. The Air Force threatened to withhold rough-
ly $4 million in incentive payments unless Northrop
tightened security even more; the company complied,
with alacrity.
"We wanted them to understand in no uncertain
terms, an official recently recalled, "that the govern-
